### Leveraging Vision-Language Models to Select Trustworthy Super-Resolution Samples Generated by Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20832v1)
- **Authors**: Cansu Korkmaz, Ahmet Murat Tekalp, Zafer Dogan
- **Abstract**: Super-resolution (SR) is an ill-posed inverse problem with many feasible solutions consistent with a given low-resolution image. On one hand, regressive SR models aim to balance fidelity and perceptual quality to yield a single solution, but this trade-off often introduces artifacts that create ambiguity in information-critical applications such as recognizing digits or letters. On the other hand, diffusion models generate a diverse set of SR images, but selecting the most trustworthy solution from this set remains a challenge. This paper introduces a robust, automated framework for identifying the most trustworthy SR sample from a diffusion-generated set by leveraging the semantic reasoning capabilities of vision-language models (VLMs). Specifically, VLMs such as BLIP-2, GPT-4o, and their variants are prompted with structured queries to assess semantic correctness, visual quality, and artifact presence. The top-ranked SR candidates are then ensembled to yield a single trustworthy output in a cost-effective manner. To rigorously assess the validity of VLM-selected samples, we propose a novel Trustworthiness Score (TWS) a hybrid metric that quantifies SR reliability based on three complementary components: semantic similarity via CLIP embeddings, structural integrity using SSIM on edge maps, and artifact sensitivity through multi-level wavelet decomposition. We empirically show that TWS correlates strongly with human preference in both ambiguous and natural images, and that VLM-guided selections consistently yield high TWS values. Compared to conventional metrics like PSNR, LPIPS, which fail to reflect information fidelity, our approach offers a principled, scalable, and generalizable solution for navigating the uncertainty of the diffusion SR space. By aligning outputs with human expectations and semantic correctness, this work sets a new benchmark for trustworthiness in generative SR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of selecting the most trustworthy super-resolution (SR) image from a set generated by diffusion models, particularly when semantic correctness is paramount (e.g., digit recognition). The authors propose a framework that leverages vision-language models (VLMs) like BLIP-2 and GPT-4o to evaluate SR candidates based on semantic correctness, visual quality, and artifact presence, using tailored prompts. The top-ranked samples are ensembled to produce a final SR output.  The paper also introduces a novel "Trustworthiness Score" (TWS) – a hybrid metric combining semantic similarity (CLIP embeddings), structural integrity (edge-based SSIM), and artifact sensitivity (wavelet decomposition) – to quantify SR reliability and validate VLM selections.  Experiments demonstrate that VLM-guided selection yields higher TWS values and aligns well with human preferences compared to traditional SR metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The key novelty lies in the *integration of VLMs for automated selection of trustworthy SR samples from diffusion models*. Prior work often relied on pixel-wise metrics or human-in-the-loop approaches for this selection, which is costly and subjective. The TWS metric is also a novel contribution as it combines semantic understanding with structural integrity and artifact assessment, which more accurately reflects SR reliability. The idea of using specific prompts to guide the VLMs is also novel and essential to the framework's success.
*   **Weaknesses:** While leveraging VLMs is innovative in this specific SR context, VLMs have been applied in other image evaluation tasks. The fundamental components of the TWS (CLIP, SSIM, wavelet decomposition) are individually established techniques. The novelty is in their combination and specific application to trustworthiness in SR.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper's significance lies in its ability to address a crucial problem in SR: *resolving ambiguity and selecting reliable outputs when semantic correctness is critical*. This has implications for downstream tasks like OCR, medical image analysis, and other information-critical applications. The work offers a more principled and scalable alternative to relying solely on perceptual quality metrics, which often fail to capture subtle but important semantic errors. The human evaluation helps establish trust in the system.
*   **Weaknesses:** The experimental results, while promising, are primarily focused on digit/letter recognition and natural images. A stronger evaluation could include results on medical images, satellite images, or other niche application areas where trustworthy SR is of paramount importance to demonstrate generalizability. The computational cost of running the VLMs is not fully addressed, though the authors mention the cost-effectiveness of their approach compared to human feedback. Also, the performance hinges on the choice of prompts, which can be seen as somewhat ad-hoc, although the paper explores this sensitivity.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign this paper a score of **7**.

*   It presents a genuinely novel combination of techniques (VLMs and diffusion models for trustworthy SR sample selection), addressing a real-world problem with a clearly defined solution.
*   The TWS metric and the experimental results provide strong empirical support for the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
*   The method is demonstrated to align well with human preferences and surpass traditional SR metrics in trustworthiness.

However:

*   The individual components of the TWS are not revolutionary.
*   The generalizability and limitations of the prompt engineering strategy and the VLM performance should be analyzed further.
*   A broader range of experimental datasets, specifically related to the potential real-world applications, would increase the impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leaner Training, Lower Leakage: Revisiting Memorization in LLM Fine-Tuning with LoRA
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20856v1)
- **Authors**: Fei Wang, Baochun Li
- **Abstract**: Memorization in large language models (LLMs) makes them vulnerable to data extraction attacks. While pre-training memorization has been extensively studied, fewer works have explored its impact in fine-tuning, particularly for LoRA fine-tuning, a widely adopted parameter-efficient method. In this work, we re-examine memorization in fine-tuning and uncover a surprising divergence from prior findings across different fine-tuning strategies. Factors such as model scale and data duplication, which strongly influence memorization in pre-training and full fine-tuning, do not follow the same trend in LoRA fine-tuning. Using a more relaxed similarity-based memorization metric, we demonstrate that LoRA significantly reduces memorization risks compared to full fine-tuning, while still maintaining strong task performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper revisits the problem of memorization in Large Language Models (LLMs), focusing on the impact of different fine-tuning strategies. It specifically investigates LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) fine-tuning, a parameter-efficient technique, and compares it to full fine-tuning and head-only fine-tuning. The authors find that LoRA significantly reduces memorization risks compared to full fine-tuning while maintaining strong task performance. They achieve this by using a plagiarism-based metric and a more relaxed similarity-based metric. They also explore the effects of model scale, data duplication, and LoRA hyperparameters on memorization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the empirical analysis of memorization in LoRA fine-tuning, an area that hasn't been extensively studied. The comparison to full and head-only fine-tuning is valuable, and the discovery that LoRA exhibits different memorization patterns compared to prior findings in those strategies is a key contribution. The use of both a plagiarism-based metric *and* a sentence similarity metric offers a more complete picture of memorization.

*   **Significance:** Memorization in LLMs is a crucial security and privacy concern. The finding that LoRA can significantly reduce memorization risks while maintaining performance is important for practitioners who want to fine-tune LLMs on potentially sensitive data. This can potentially allow for broader application of LLMs in scenarios where privacy is paramount.  The investigation into how LoRA hyperparameters affect memorization provides practical guidance for mitigating these risks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the different fine-tuning methods and memorization metrics.
    *   The experiments are well-designed and use multiple GPT-2 model sizes, a newer Llama 3 model and multiple datasets (Arxiver and ChatDoctor).
    *   The use of both plagiarism-based metrics and sentence similarity provides a more comprehensive view.
    *   Investigation of LoRA hyperparameters adds practical value.
    *   Results show that LoRA consistently yields significantly less memorization when compared to full-fine tuning, without sacrificing too much performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the empirical findings are valuable, the paper lacks a deep *theoretical* explanation of why LoRA reduces memorization. This leaves room for future work. While the authors do suggest that LoRA's parameter-efficient design restricts excessive retention of fine-tuning data, this is descriptive rather than explanatory.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on a black-box data extraction scenario. It would be valuable to explore how LoRA affects more advanced data extraction attacks, even if only speculatively.
    *   The use of GPT-2 models in the evaluation, while helpful for controlled comparisons, is a little dated.  While the authors do include experiments with Llama 3.2 model, the experiments are limited to varying the hyperparameters.
    *   There are no theoretical guarantees for data security, even though empirical results suggest a better method with LoRA.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in LLM fine-tuning and memorization. It provides a strong argument for using LoRA as a privacy-preserving fine-tuning technique. It encourages further research into the theoretical properties of LoRA that lead to reduced memorization. It also highlights the importance of using multiple memorization metrics to get a more complete picture.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper provides a valuable empirical investigation into an important problem, namely memorization in LLM fine-tuning. The finding that LoRA significantly reduces memorization risks is novel and practically relevant.  The thorough evaluation and clear presentation strengthen the paper. However, the lack of a deeper theoretical explanation and limited exploration of more advanced attacks prevent it from achieving a higher score. Furthermore, while Llama experiments are included, there is still a heavy focus on using the GPT-2 architecture for fine-tuning.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Engineering RAG Systems for Real-World Applications: Design, Development, and Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20869v1)
- **Authors**: Md Toufique Hasan, Muhammad Waseem, Kai-Kristian Kemell, Ayman Asad Khan, Mika Saari, Pekka Abrahamsson
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are emerging as a key approach for grounding Large Language Models (LLMs) in external knowledge, addressing limitations in factual accuracy and contextual relevance. However, there is a lack of empirical studies that report on the development of RAG-based implementations grounded in real-world use cases, evaluated through general user involvement, and accompanied by systematic documentation of lessons learned. This paper presents five domain-specific RAG applications developed for real-world scenarios across governance, cybersecurity, agriculture, industrial research, and medical diagnostics. Each system incorporates multilingual OCR, semantic retrieval via vector embeddings, and domain-adapted LLMs, deployed through local servers or cloud APIs to meet distinct user needs. A web-based evaluation involving a total of 100 participants assessed the systems across six dimensions: (i) Ease of Use, (ii) Relevance, (iii) Transparency, (iv) Responsiveness, (v) Accuracy, and (vi) Likelihood of Recommendation. Based on user feedback and our development experience, we documented twelve key lessons learned, highlighting technical, operational, and ethical challenges affecting the reliability and usability of RAG systems in practice.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Engineering RAG Systems for Real-World Applications: Design, Development, and Evaluation" presents an empirical study on the development and evaluation of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems across five diverse real-world application domains: municipal governance, cybersecurity, agriculture, industrial research, and medical diagnostics. The authors detail the design and implementation of the RAG pipelines, highlighting technical challenges such as OCR noise, domain-specific model selection, and chunk size optimization.  A user study involving 100 participants was conducted to evaluate the systems based on ease of use, relevance, transparency, responsiveness, accuracy, and recommendation. The paper concludes with twelve lessons learned, covering technical, operational, and ethical aspects of building RAG systems for real-world applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Focus on Real-World Applications:** The paper's primary strength is its focus on deploying and evaluating RAG systems in practical, real-world scenarios.  Many RAG papers focus on benchmark datasets; this paper's empirical investigation provides valuable insights that are often missed by more theoretical or benchmark-driven research.
    *   **Diverse Application Domains:**  The breadth of application domains (governance, cybersecurity, agriculture, research, healthcare) allows for a more generalizable understanding of RAG system behavior across different contexts.
    *   **User-Centered Evaluation:**  The inclusion of a user study provides valuable insights into user perceptions of RAG system usability, trust, and effectiveness, complementing technical performance metrics.
    *   **Lessons Learned:**  The distilled "lessons learned" offer practical guidance for researchers and practitioners building RAG systems, encapsulating real-world challenges and mitigation strategies. This section is very strong.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Depth of Technical Innovation:** The paper's novelty in terms of *technical innovation* in RAG architecture is limited. While the application of RAG is novel in the specific domains and in the combination across domains, the core RAG components themselves aren't significantly advanced beyond the existing literature.
    *   **Limited Detail on Specific Implementations:** While the paper outlines the overall architecture and components, it could benefit from providing more detailed information on the specific implementation choices for each domain.  For example, further elaboration on the prompt engineering strategies or specific data cleaning techniques employed in each domain would be valuable.
    *   **Generalizability Concerns (Demographics):** While the paper mentions the mix of professionals, students, and domain experts, the demographics still pose a threat to generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Quantification Limitations:** The user study is well-structured but relies heavily on Likert scales. While open-ended feedback is included, the analysis could have benefited from more quantitative measures, particularly in assessing the accuracy of responses or retrieval relevance.
    *   **Evaluation Agent Module:** Mentioned in the conclusion, the idea of the "Evaluation Agent Model" is presented but not evaluated, decreasing the novelty of its contribution.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper's novelty lies primarily in its *application* of RAG to diverse real-world domains and its systematic, user-centered evaluation. While individual components (e.g., OCR, embeddings, LLMs) are not novel, the end-to-end integration and the focus on practical challenges and lessons learned make a useful contribution.
    *   The significance of the paper stems from its ability to bridge the gap between theoretical RAG research and real-world deployment. The practical insights offered by the lessons learned can inform future research directions and guide the development of more robust and trustworthy RAG systems.
    *   The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of AI in engineering.

*Score: 6.5*

**Justification:**

A score of 6.5 reflects a balanced assessment of the paper's strengths and weaknesses. While the technical innovation is limited, the paper excels in its empirical focus on real-world applications, user-centered evaluation, and practical lessons learned. The diversity of application domains and the thoroughness of the study contribute to the paper's significance in the field. The paper would be improved with more in-depth technical details on each domain’s implementation and by providing quantitative measurement, to a greater degree, alongside the surveys. The paper's contribution to software engineering and the engineering of AI systems is solid and contributes to the movement towards more real-world and usable RAG systems.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### MultiHuman-Testbench: Benchmarking Image Generation for Multiple Humans
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20879v1)
- **Authors**: Shubhankar Borse, Seokeon Choi, Sunghyun Park, Jeongho Kim, Shreya Kadambi, Risheek Garrepalli, Sungrack Yun, Munawar Hayat, Fatih Porikli
- **Abstract**: Generation of images containing multiple humans, performing complex actions, while preserving their facial identities, is a significant challenge. A major factor contributing to this is the lack of a a dedicated benchmark. To address this, we introduce MultiHuman-Testbench, a novel benchmark for rigorously evaluating generative models for multi-human generation. The benchmark comprises 1800 samples, including carefully curated text prompts, describing a range of simple to complex human actions. These prompts are matched with a total of 5,550 unique human face images, sampled uniformly to ensure diversity across age, ethnic background, and gender. Alongside captions, we provide human-selected pose conditioning images which accurately match the prompt. We propose a multi-faceted evaluation suite employing four key metrics to quantify face count, ID similarity, prompt alignment, and action detection. We conduct a thorough evaluation of a diverse set of models, including zero-shot approaches and training-based methods, with and without regional priors. We also propose novel techniques to incorporate image and region isolation using human segmentation and Hungarian matching, significantly improving ID similarity. Our proposed benchmark and key findings provide valuable insights and a standardized tool for advancing research in multi-human image generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MultiHuman-Testbench, a new benchmark for evaluating generative models designed to create images with multiple humans. The benchmark consists of 1800 samples, including text prompts describing various actions and 5,550 unique human face images, ensuring diversity in age, ethnicity, and gender. The paper also provides pose conditioning images alongside captions. A multi-faceted evaluation suite is proposed, using metrics to quantify face count, ID similarity, prompt alignment, and action detection.  The authors evaluate several models, including zero-shot and training-based methods, with and without regional priors, and they also introduce novel techniques to improve ID similarity using human segmentation and Hungarian matching. The paper aims to provide a standardized tool and valuable insights for advancing multi-human image generation research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary contribution of this paper lies in the creation of the MultiHuman-Testbench dataset and the associated evaluation metrics.  The lack of a dedicated benchmark for multi-human image generation has been a recognized limitation. Introducing a large-scale, diverse dataset with carefully curated prompts and annotations represents a significant step forward. The proposed evaluation metrics, which go beyond basic image quality to assess ID preservation, action understanding, and prompt alignment, also add to the novelty. The proposed methods to improve identity consistency are incremental over existing techniques but contribute to a practical improvement.

* **Significance:**  The significance stems from the potential impact on research in multi-human image generation. By providing a common platform for evaluation, the benchmark will likely accelerate progress in this challenging area. The benchmark is especially valuable because it includes challenging aspects, like identity consistency, which are often overlooked in existing evaluations of generative models.  The analysis of existing models exposes clear shortcomings and biases, indicating areas where future research should focus. The analysis reveals the need for improved ID preservation in uncontrolled environments, and addressing these biases is ethically important.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Large-scale, diverse dataset:** The dataset is a crucial asset and will be welcomed by the community.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation metrics:** The evaluation metrics are well-designed and relevant to the specific challenges of multi-human generation.
    * **Thorough evaluation of existing models:** The paper provides valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches.
    * **Open source and reproducible:** The details of the approach, and (presumably) the dataset will be available, furthering adoption and impact.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental methodological contributions:**  While the proposed regional isolation techniques improve identity consistency, they build upon existing work in regional prompting and attention mechanisms. The novelty of the methods themselves is somewhat limited. The use of Hungarian matching is a fairly standard optimization technique.
    * **Limited analysis of failure cases:** While the paper discusses general trends, a more in-depth analysis of specific failure modes would strengthen the impact. For example, further analysis could explore why certain models perform better or worse on specific types of actions or demographic groups.
    * **Dependence on existing models:** The use of models like SAM, ArcFace, and Gemini Flash is standard but relies on the continued performance and availability of these components.
    * **The lack of code availability:** It is uncertain if code for MH-Omnigen will be released. Without it, reproducibility is difficult.

* **Potential Influence:**  The benchmark is likely to become a standard for evaluating multi-human image generation models.  The identification of biases in existing models will stimulate research into more fair and equitable generative systems.  The insights gained from evaluating different approaches will guide future research directions, focusing on areas where significant improvements are needed.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. The creation of the dataset and associated benchmark is a substantial contribution that addresses a key gap in the field. The evaluation provides valuable insights into the performance of existing models, and highlights important limitations and biases. However, the algorithmic contributions are somewhat incremental. The value and importance of the dataset, and broadness of the analysis offset the incremental nature of the methodological advances.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Omniwise: Predicting GPU Kernels Performance with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20886v1)
- **Authors**: Zixian Wang, Cole Ramos, Muhammad A. Awad, Keith Lowery
- **Abstract**: In recent years, the rapid advancement of deep neural networks (DNNs) has revolutionized artificial intelligence, enabling models with unprecedented capabilities in understanding, generating, and processing complex data. These powerful architectures have transformed a wide range of downstream applications, tackling tasks beyond human reach. In this paper, we introduce Omniwise, the first end-to-end, self-supervised fine-tuning pipeline that applies large language models (LLMs) to GPU kernel performance prediction--a novel use case in performance profiling. Omniwise is model-agnostic and lightweight, achieving strong results even with a small 3B-parameter model. It can predict key performance metrics, including memory bandwidth, cache hit rates, GFLOPs, and arithmetic intensity, directly from kernel code without the need for code execution or profiling tools. Our approach achieves over 90% of predictions within 10% relative error on GPU kernels executed on AMD MI250 and MI300X architectures. In addition to the pipeline, we develop an online inference server and a Visual Studio Code plugin that seamlessly integrate LLM-based performance prediction into developers' workflows.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Omniwise: Predicting GPU Kernels Performance with LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Omniwise, a novel end-to-end pipeline that uses large language models (LLMs) to predict GPU kernel performance. Omniwise aims to circumvent the time-consuming and expertise-demanding process of traditional GPU performance profiling. Instead of running kernels and collecting hardware counters, it leverages a fine-tuned LLAMA 3.2 3B model to predict performance counters directly from the source code. The pipeline includes data generation and augmentation (using synthetic kernels, AI-generated code, and custom kernels), performance counter collection, model training, and a Visual Studio Code extension for serving the model to developers. The authors claim that Omniwise achieves high accuracy (over 90% of predictions within 10% relative error) on AMD MI250 and MI300X architectures, making it a practical alternative to traditional profiling workflows.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the application of LLMs for GPU kernel performance prediction *directly from the source code*. While other works have used LLMs for code generation or compiler optimization, the direct prediction of performance counters to this degree, especially across diverse hardware and software configurations and without explicit code execution/instrumentation, seems to be a relatively new approach.  The end-to-end pipeline, including data generation and the VS Code integration, also adds to the novelty. The specific data augmentation strategies also provide some novel implementation details, although they are based on well-established augmentation techniques. The comparison with Meta's LLM compiler also clarifies the novelty that Omniwise's contribution is correlating the source code to performance counter while Meta's one is about LLVM intermediate representations.

**Significance:**  If the claimed accuracy and speed hold true in real-world scenarios, Omniwise could significantly impact GPU software development. The benefits are considerable:
*   **Reduced development time:** Developers can get immediate feedback on performance implications of code changes, rather than waiting for profiling cycles.
*   **Democratization of performance optimization:**  It lowers the barrier to entry for optimizing GPU code, as it reduces reliance on deep microarchitecture expertise.
*   **Early-stage optimization:** Performance can be considered even before complete code development and debugging are finished.
*   **Design Space Exploration:** Omniwise may be used to explore GPU kernel performance and hardware-specific code optimizations.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive pipeline:**  The paper presents a complete and well-defined workflow, from data generation to deployment.
*   **Strong results:** The reported accuracy figures are impressive, particularly given the complexity of the prediction task. The analysis also demonstrates good accuracy across a variety of metrics.
*   **Practical integration:**  The VS Code extension adds real-world usability.
*   **Extensive Dataset:** The paper builds an extensive datasets (over 955,000 HIP kernels).
*   **Clear writing and organization:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited generalizability demonstrated:** The evaluation is focused on AMD GPUs (MI250 and MI300X) and HIP. The question of how well Omniwise generalizes to other architectures (e.g., NVIDIA GPUs or other platforms) is largely unanswered. The model may need substantial retraining.
*   **Simplified assumptions:** The paper acknowledges the assumption of large problem sizes.  The effect of different problem sizes and the general handling of scalability is not addressed thoroughly, and this could limit the tool's applicability. Also, the assumption of single source file, clean parsing, etc. may require preprocessing for practical applications.
*   **Lack of detailed error analysis:** The paper presents aggregate accuracy metrics, but a more detailed error analysis (e.g., identifying types of kernels or code patterns where the model struggles) would be valuable.  Discussion on edge cases and model limitations would strengthen the paper.
*  **Dependence on empirically derived roofline model** the predicted performance counters are normalized to the empirically-derived roofline peak values for target hardware. While this improves the generalizability of the model across hardware generations, the underlying hardware roofline model still needs to be derived through conventional performance analysis on the specific piece of hardware.
*   **Dependence on ROCm toolchain:** Omniwise is explicitly stated to be a ROCm toolchain for empirical roofline model, which lacks of diversity in GPU acceleration and programming languages.

**Potential Influence:**

Omniwise could inspire further research into using LLMs for performance prediction, static analysis, and automated optimization. The pipeline and data generation techniques could serve as a blueprint for similar tools targeting different platforms or programming languages. The potential impact on developer productivity and the democratization of performance optimization are significant.

**Justification of Score:**

While Omniwise presents a promising approach with strong initial results, the limitations regarding generalizability and the simplified assumptions prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution at this stage. I believe the strength of the paper comes with the implementation details of data augmentation. Therefore, a score of 7 is appropriate. It represents a significant contribution with clear novelty, good results, and practical value, but with some open questions and limitations that need to be addressed in future work before its true potential can be fully realized.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### FaSTA$^*$: Fast-Slow Toolpath Agent with Subroutine Mining for Efficient Multi-turn Image Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20911v1)
- **Authors**: Advait Gupta, Rishie Raj, Dang Nguyen, Tianyi Zhou
- **Abstract**: We develop a cost-efficient neurosymbolic agent to address challenging multi-turn image editing tasks such as "Detect the bench in the image while recoloring it to pink. Also, remove the cat for a clearer view and recolor the wall to yellow.'' It combines the fast, high-level subtask planning by large language models (LLMs) with the slow, accurate, tool-use, and local A$^*$ search per subtask to find a cost-efficient toolpath -- a sequence of calls to AI tools. To save the cost of A$^*$ on similar subtasks, we perform inductive reasoning on previously successful toolpaths via LLMs to continuously extract/refine frequently used subroutines and reuse them as new tools for future tasks in an adaptive fast-slow planning, where the higher-level subroutines are explored first, and only when they fail, the low-level A$^*$ search is activated. The reusable symbolic subroutines considerably save exploration cost on the same types of subtasks applied to similar images, yielding a human-like fast-slow toolpath agent "FaSTA$^*$'': fast subtask planning followed by rule-based subroutine selection per subtask is attempted by LLMs at first, which is expected to cover most tasks, while slow A$^*$ search is only triggered for novel and challenging subtasks. By comparing with recent image editing approaches, we demonstrate FaSTA$^*$ is significantly more computationally efficient while remaining competitive with the state-of-the-art baseline in terms of success rate.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FaSTA*, a neurosymbolic agent designed for efficient multi-turn image editing.  It combines fast, high-level subtask planning using large language models (LLMs) with a slow, accurate A* search for optimal toolpath selection within each subtask.  A key innovation is the online learning and refinement of symbolic subroutines, where LLMs are used to extract and generalize frequently used tool sequences from past successful editing tasks. These learned subroutines enable "fast planning," avoiding expensive A* searches for common subtasks. When no suitable subroutine exists or a quality check fails, FaSTA* falls back to A* search for "slow planning". The method demonstrates significant computational efficiency improvements compared to existing approaches like CoSTA*, while maintaining competitive image editing quality.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the online learning of symbolic subroutines within a neurosymbolic image editing framework. While combining LLMs and A* search isn't entirely new (CoSTA* did it), the *adaptive* aspect of extracting and reusing subroutines significantly enhances efficiency and can be considered a novel contribution.  The specific application of ICRL is innovative. The inductive reasoning step leveraging LLMs to extract these reusable actions is also a clever idea that saves a lot of compute.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key bottleneck in multi-turn image editing – the computational cost of toolpath search. By demonstrating a method that significantly reduces this cost while preserving quality, FaSTA* represents a substantial step toward more practical and scalable image editing agents. The idea of learning and reusing editing "macros" is intuitive and likely to be influential. The potential for FaSTA* to be combined with the new Gemini Ultra Large Multimodal model is interesting and likely to improve FaSTA*s performance even further.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of existing methods and motivates the need for a more efficient approach.
    *   **Well-defined method:** The FaSTA* framework is clearly described, with detailed explanations of each component and how they interact.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, with comparisons to strong baselines (especially CoSTA*), ablation studies, and qualitative results.  The inclusion of a held-out dataset for testing the generalizability of learned subroutines is a good practice.
    *   **Significant performance improvements:** The paper demonstrates substantial improvements in execution time with minimal quality degradation. The Pareto optimality analysis provides additional evidence of the method's effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on LLM performance:** Like many neurosymbolic approaches, FaSTA* relies on the quality of LLM-generated subtask plans and extractions. Performance could be limited by the reliability and expressiveness of the LLM. The prompts that were used for training the LLM and the selection parameters for all subroutines could have been expanded to more complex tasks. The paper did not specify how well FaSTA* performed when presented with edge-case tasks like editing multiple objects in the same image or creating new scenes in an image.
    *   **Cold start problem:** The method likely suffers from a "cold start" problem, where its performance is initially limited until sufficient experiences are gathered to learn effective subroutines. The method of combating "cold start" may have been limited as the LLM would have already been trained with specific datasets that would cause a bias in subroutine learning.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's ideas have the potential to influence future research in neurosymbolic AI, tool-using agents, and image editing.  The concept of learning and reusing subroutines could be applied to other tasks beyond image editing. The method also provides a concrete approach for integrating ICRL principles in existing visual models. The adaptive fast-slow structure is useful for future visual-editing tasks that involve low- and high-compute problems.

*   **Score Justification:** Considering its novelty in subroutine extraction and adaptation for efficient tool use, its comprehensive evaluation, and potential influence, the paper makes a solid contribution. However, limitations surrounding reliance on LLM reliability and potential "cold start" impacts prevent it from being exceptional.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ZKPROV: A Zero-Knowledge Approach to Dataset Provenance for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20915v1)
- **Authors**: Mina Namazi, Alexander Nemecek, Erman Ayday
- **Abstract**: As the deployment of large language models (LLMs) grows in sensitive domains, ensuring the integrity of their computational provenance becomes a critical challenge, particularly in regulated sectors such as healthcare, where strict requirements are applied in dataset usage. We introduce ZKPROV, a novel cryptographic framework that enables zero-knowledge proofs of LLM provenance. It allows users to verify that a model is trained on a reliable dataset without revealing sensitive information about it or its parameters. Unlike prior approaches that focus on complete verification of the training process (incurring significant computational cost) or depend on trusted execution environments, ZKPROV offers a distinct balance. Our method cryptographically binds a trained model to its authorized training dataset(s) through zero-knowledge proofs while avoiding proof of every training step. By leveraging dataset-signed metadata and compact model parameter commitments, ZKPROV provides sound and privacy-preserving assurances that the result of the LLM is derived from a model trained on the claimed authorized and relevant dataset. Experimental results demonstrate the efficiency and scalability of the ZKPROV in generating this proof and verifying it, achieving a practical solution for real-world deployments. We also provide formal security guarantees, proving that our approach preserves dataset confidentiality while ensuring trustworthy dataset provenance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ZKPROV paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ZKPROV: A Zero-Knowledge Approach to Dataset Provenance for Large Language Models" introduces a novel cryptographic framework called ZKPROV that allows users to verify that a large language model (LLM) was trained on a specific, authorized dataset without revealing sensitive information about the dataset or the model parameters. ZKPROV uses zero-knowledge proofs to cryptographically bind a trained model to its authorized training data. The framework leverages dataset-signed metadata and compact model parameter commitments, providing assurances that the LLM is derived from a model trained on the claimed authorized dataset. Experimental results demonstrate the efficiency and scalability of ZKPROV.  The paper also provides formal security guarantees, proving the preservation of dataset confidentiality and ensuring trustworthy dataset provenance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in addressing the specific problem of dataset provenance in LLMs using zero-knowledge proofs, focusing on *binding* a model to training data rather than just verifying training or inference correctness (as most prior work does). This is a meaningful contribution, particularly in regulated sectors like healthcare.  The use of a recursive SNARK (HyperNova) to efficiently handle the constraints arising from this binding is also a valuable engineering contribution.  The combination of BLS signatures, KZG commitments, and Reckle trees is a sophisticated combination of existing tools, adapted well to the problem.

* **Significance:** The ability to verify dataset provenance is extremely significant for deploying LLMs in sensitive domains where regulatory compliance is essential.  The work directly tackles a practical need that existing verifiability systems for ML don't fully address. The paper clearly articulates a real-world need in medical contexts and provides a solution that could potentially improve trust in LLM deployments. While the paper focuses on fine-tuning scenarios, the applicability to pre-training scenarios increases the broad significance. The results, showing sub-second proof generation for reasonable-sized LLMs, demonstrate the feasibility of the approach.

* **Strengths:**
    * Addresses an important and previously underexplored problem (dataset provenance).
    * Provides a practical cryptographic framework with concrete implementation and experimental results.
    * Offers formal security guarantees for confidentiality and soundness.
    * Employs state-of-the-art cryptographic techniques efficiently.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * Performance: Although sub-second for single dataset models, scalability for multi-dataset models requires further optimization (superlinear growth of proving time).
    * Limited Comparisons: The paper lacks direct comparisons with other (albeit non-existent) *dataset provenance* schemes. The comparison with zkLLM, ZEN, etc., highlights their focus on other aspects (training verification), but it's crucial to consider any potential baselines, or discuss the modifications needed to apply other methods.
    * Reliance on Strong Cryptographic Assumptions: Like any crypto system, it relies on the standard assumptions that underpin KZG, BLS and HyperNova. While these are widely used assumptions, a discussion about alternative constructions with different tradeoffs might improve the paper.
    * Scope: The paper primarily focuses on guaranteeing dataset provenance *at training time* but does not prevent data from being introduced during inference (e.g., through Retrieval Augmented Generation, or RAG). A discussion of how ZKPROV can be integrated into a RAG-based LLM system, or modified to prove the provenance of both training data *and* inference data, would be beneficial and relevant for real-world deployments.
    * The paper does not address the computational cost incurred by the owner of datasets i.e., "Authority CA", to generate public parameters for the entire framework.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of more trustworthy and auditable LLM deployment practices, especially in regulated industries. The work opens up a new area of research on verifiable machine learning, focusing on dataset provenance.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper addresses a critical gap in verifiable machine learning and makes a practical contribution, the limitations in multi-dataset scaling, limited comparative analysis, and the specific (training only) scope keep it from a higher score. The novelty is significant, but not groundbreaking. The importance is high, but the current solution has scaling limitations.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Metadata Enrichment of Long Text Documents using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20918v1)
- **Authors**: Manika Lamba, You Peng, Sophie Nikolov, Glen Layne-Worthey, J. Stephen Downie
- **Abstract**: In this project, we semantically enriched and enhanced the metadata of long text documents, theses and dissertations, retrieved from the HathiTrust Digital Library in English published from 1920 to 2020 through a combination of manual efforts and large language models. This dataset provides a valuable resource for advancing research in areas such as computational social science, digital humanities, and information science. Our paper shows that enriching metadata using LLMs is particularly beneficial for digital repositories by introducing additional metadata access points that may not have originally been foreseen to accommodate various content types. This approach is particularly effective for repositories that have significant missing data in their existing metadata fields, enhancing search results and improving the accessibility of the digital repository.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper explores a hybrid approach to metadata enrichment for long-text documents (theses and dissertations) from the HathiTrust Digital Library. The method combines manual data cleaning and correction with automated techniques using Large Language Models (LLMs) for semantic enrichment. The researchers focus on enriching existing metadata fields, adding new fields (such as advisor, committee members, and abstract), and using LLMs to extract keywords and generate abstracts.  They highlight the challenges encountered during the process, including issues with multiple authors/degrees, name formatting, duplicate entries, and OCR errors. The authors emphasize the benefits of LLMs for introducing additional metadata access points that are suitable for varied content types, which contributes to improved search and accessibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of manual data curation and LLM-based semantic enrichment is not entirely novel, but the paper's focus on *long text documents* (ETDs) and the specific challenges associated with their metadata within a large digital library like HathiTrust adds a valuable contribution. The paper clearly outlines the practical implementation of such a hybrid approach, detailing the tools (KeyLLM, PaperQA) and methodologies employed. While other studies have explored metadata enrichment using LLMs, this work distinguishes itself by documenting a systematic and detailed workflow tailored to addressing the specific metadata inadequacies of a dissertation database.

*   **Significance:** The paper has several aspects of significance. First, it addresses a common problem in digital libraries: incomplete and inconsistent metadata, especially for non-book materials.  Second, it demonstrates how LLMs can be effectively used to augment manual efforts, which may be of use in smaller institutions with restricted resources. This is especially important as digital repositories grow in volume.  Third, the authors release their dataset, providing a valuable resource for other researchers working in related areas. Finally, it promotes a workflow easily adapted and implemented on similar metadata enhancement endeavors.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical approach:** The paper offers a detailed and practical guide to metadata enrichment using LLMs, with specific examples and tools.
    *   **Data release:** The availability of the enriched dataset allows for further research and validation of the methods used.
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing metadata in digital libraries and the potential of LLMs to address these issues.
    *   **Well-structured and documented methodology:** The methodology section includes detailed descriptions of the data collection, processing, and cleaning steps.
    *   **Rigorous cleaning of existing Metadata:** The authors highlight the complexity and effort involved in preparing the dataset, from removing non-dissertation entries and duplicated entries to standardizing names and degrees.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited quantitative evaluation:** While the paper discusses the process of randomly sampling and manually reviewing 10% of the metadata, it could benefit from a more extensive and formal quantitative evaluation of the quality improvements achieved by the LLM-based enrichment. Metrics such as precision, recall, and F1-score could be used to assess the accuracy of keyword extraction and abstract generation.
    *   **LLM Selection Justification:** The authors could provide a stronger justification for choosing specific LLMs (GPT-40-mini, KeyBERT, KeyLLM) over alternatives. A comparative analysis of different LLMs for these tasks would have strengthened the paper.
    *   **Generalizability:** The paper's focus on HathiTrust may limit its generalizability to other types of digital libraries with different metadata structures and content.
    *   **Lack of error analysis:** The paper mentions OCR errors and other issues encountered during data processing, but a more detailed analysis of the types and sources of these errors would be beneficial.

**Conclusion:**

The paper presents a solid and useful application of LLMs for metadata enrichment, particularly in the context of long-text documents within a large digital library. The emphasis on a hybrid approach, combining manual and automated techniques, is practical and relevant.  While some aspects could be strengthened with more rigorous quantitative evaluation and a broader discussion of generalizability, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper scores a 7.5 based on its practical value, detailed methodology, and the public availability of its dataset. It's a solid contribution, above average, demonstrating the benefits of hybrid approach and offers a systematic way to enhance digital libraries. However, the limitations in the quantitative evaluation of the LLM's performance and the justification of LLM selection keep it from reaching a higher score. Further improvement to quantify the improvements using more common LLM metrics would significantly strengthen the claims of significant enhancement. The score reflects the balance between the paper's strengths in practical application and the weaknesses regarding quantitative assessment and generalizability.

- **Classification**: cs.DL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FineWeb2: One Pipeline to Scale Them All -- Adapting Pre-Training Data Processing to Every Language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20920v1)
- **Authors**: Guilherme Penedo, Hynek Kydlíček, Vinko Sabolčec, Bettina Messmer, Negar Foroutan, Amir Hossein Kargaran, Colin Raffel, Martin Jaggi, Leandro Von Werra, Thomas Wolf
- **Abstract**: Pre-training state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) requires vast amounts of clean and diverse text data. While the open development of large high-quality English pre-training datasets has seen substantial recent progress, training performant multilingual LLMs remains a challenge, in large part due to the inherent difficulty of tailoring filtering and deduplication pipelines to a large number of languages. In this work, we introduce a new pre-training dataset curation pipeline based on FineWeb that can be automatically adapted to support any language. We extensively ablate our pipeline design choices on a set of nine diverse languages, guided by a set of meaningful and informative evaluation tasks that were chosen through a novel selection process based on measurable criteria. Ultimately, we show that our pipeline can be used to create non-English corpora that produce more performant models than prior datasets. We additionally introduce a straightforward and principled approach to rebalance datasets that takes into consideration both duplication count and quality, providing an additional performance uplift. Finally, we scale our pipeline to over 1000 languages using almost 100 Common Crawl snapshots to produce FineWeb2, a new 20 terabyte (5 billion document) multilingual dataset which we release along with our pipeline, training, and evaluation codebases.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided paper. Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FineWeb2, a new multilingual pre-training dataset curation pipeline designed to automatically adapt to any language. Building on the FineWeb approach for English, the authors address the challenge of creating high-quality multilingual corpora by developing a pipeline that tailors filtering and deduplication steps based on language-specific statistics. They extensively ablate pipeline design choices on nine diverse "canary" languages, guided by meaningful evaluation tasks selected through a novel, criteria-based process. They demonstrate that their pipeline produces non-English corpora that result in more performant models compared to those trained on prior datasets. The paper also presents a principled approach to dataset rebalancing (rehydration) based on duplication count and quality. Finally, they scale the pipeline to over 1000 languages, creating FineWeb2, a 20TB dataset that's released along with the pipeline code, training scripts, and evaluation codebases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a significant problem:** Creating high-quality, diverse, and clean multilingual pre-training data is a major bottleneck in developing performant multilingual LLMs.
    *   **Novelty in Adaptability:** The core contribution of an automatically adaptable pipeline is valuable. It moves beyond the "one-size-fits-all" approach of many existing multilingual datasets, which can lead to suboptimal filtering for individual languages.
    *   **Data-Driven Approach:** The extensive ablation studies, using a set of diverse languages and carefully selected evaluation tasks, provide strong evidence for the efficacy of the pipeline design choices. The data-driven aspect adds a layer of rigor often missing in dataset creation papers.
    *   **Principled Rebalancing (Rehydration):** The duplication-aware upsampling scheme based on the quality assessment and original duplication count information is a creative solution to improve performance.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework:** The paper introduces a novel methodology for evaluation task selection and quantitatively assess the task.
    *   **Resource Release:** Releasing the code, datasets, and evaluation framework significantly enhances the impact of the work, enabling further research and development in multilingual LLMs.
    *   **Impressive Scale:** The scale of FineWeb2 (20TB, 1000+ languages) is a substantial contribution.
    *   **Open availability:** Releasing the data under a permissive license (ODC-By) further promotes accessibility and collaboration.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Existing Components:** While the adaptation strategy is novel, the underlying techniques (e.g., MinHash, FastText LID) are well-established. The contribution lies in the application and adaptation rather than fundamental algorithmic breakthroughs.
    *   **"Canary" Language Bias:** The pipeline design is driven by ablations on nine languages. The generalization of these design choices to the remaining 990+ languages is a concern, although the "unseen languages" validation provides some reassurance.
    *   **Low-Resource Language Data Issues:** The acknowledgment of limitations in the low-resource corpora (e.g., dominance of Bible/Wikipedia content) points to an inherent challenge in web-scraped datasets. The proposed wordlist filtering can only mitigate, not eliminate, this.
    *   **Adaptive Filter may result in Overfitting:** the evaluation in multiple languages might cause the filter to be overfitted, it is a weakness of the method but it is difficult to validate.
    *   **Limited novelty of the final result**: The paper focuses on the creation of a tool instead of a performance improvement, it is a major constraint given that all evaluation is compared against similar techniques.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper has the potential to significantly impact the development of multilingual LLMs. The adaptable pipeline offers a practical solution to a key data curation challenge. The released dataset serves as a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners. However, the incremental nature of the technical contributions (building on existing techniques) and the acknowledgment of data quality limitations in very low-resource languages temper the overall impact.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7.5 is appropriate. While the core techniques aren't groundbreaking, the *systematic approach to adaptation, the data-driven validation, the effective rebalancing strategy, the released resources, and the sheer scale of the resulting dataset* represent a significant, valuable contribution to the field of multilingual NLP. The novelty lies primarily in the engineering and systematic approach to data curation, rather than a fundamental breakthrough in NLP algorithms. The limitations regarding reliance on existing techniques, biases due to the choice of "canary" languages, the difficulty of evaluating a 1000+ dataset, the limitation with bible translation and the lack of comparison against comparable techniques limits the overall impact.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CodeGuard: A Generalized and Stealthy Backdoor Watermarking for Generative Code Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20926v1)
- **Authors**: Haoxuan Li, Jiale Zhang, Xiaobing Sun, Xiapu Luo
- **Abstract**: Generative code models (GCMs) significantly enhance development efficiency through automated code generation and code summarization. However, building and training these models require computational resources and time, necessitating effective digital copyright protection to prevent unauthorized leaks and misuse. Backdoor watermarking, by embedding hidden identifiers, simplifies copyright verification by breaking the model's black-box nature. Current backdoor watermarking techniques face two main challenges: first, limited generalization across different tasks and datasets, causing fluctuating verification rates; second, insufficient stealthiness, as watermarks are easily detected and removed by automated methods. To address these issues, we propose CodeGuard, a novel watermarking method combining attention mechanisms with distributed trigger embedding strategies. Specifically, CodeGuard employs attention mechanisms to identify watermark embedding positions, ensuring verifiability. Moreover, by using homomorphic character replacement, it avoids manual detection, while distributed trigger embedding reduces the likelihood of automated detection. Experimental results demonstrate that CodeGuard achieves up to 100% watermark verification rates in both code summarization and code generation tasks, with no impact on the primary task performance. In terms of stealthiness, CodeGuard performs exceptionally, with a maximum detection rate of only 0.078 against ONION detection methods, significantly lower than baseline methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CodeGuard, a novel backdoor watermarking method for generative code models (GCMs). CodeGuard aims to improve upon existing techniques by enhancing both the stealthiness and generalizability of the watermarks. It combines an attention mechanism for identifying optimal watermark embedding positions with a distributed trigger embedding strategy that uses homomorphic character replacement to avoid detection. The attention mechanism ensures verifiability across different tasks and datasets, while the distributed embedding increases stealth. The experimental results demonstrate that CodeGuard achieves high watermark verification rates with minimal impact on the primary task and exhibits superior stealth compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of techniques, but its core novelty lies in the specific application of attention mechanisms for selecting embedding positions and the use of homomorphic character replacement within a distributed embedding strategy to achieve stealth. The idea of using attention mechanisms to identify important parts of code is interesting and has the potential to be widely used. The distributed embedding with homoglyphs is an incremental improvement over existing techniques, which often focus on either fixed vocabulary or simple semantic transformations.
*   **Significance:** The problem of copyright protection for generative models is becoming increasingly important, especially as these models become more powerful and resource-intensive to train. The paper addresses a critical need in this domain by providing a practical method for watermarking code models.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **High verification rates:** Achieving nearly 100% verification rates is a significant accomplishment.
    *   **Minimal impact on task performance:** Demonstrating that the watermark doesn't degrade the model's primary function is crucial for practical adoption.
    *   **Superior stealth:** The experimental results show that CodeGuard outperforms existing methods in terms of stealth, making it more difficult for attackers to detect and remove the watermark.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper includes thorough experiments across two generative tasks, two datasets, and multiple evaluation metrics.
    *   **Addresses limitations of existing methods:** The paper clearly identifies and addresses the shortcomings of previous approaches, such as CoProtector's poor stealth and CodeMark's limited applicability.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Adversarial Testing:** The paper would benefit from exploring adversarial attacks to test the robustness of CodeGuard against active watermark removal attempts. The spectral signature and ONION detectors are relatively simple.
    *   **Scalability concerns to larger models (LLMs):** While the results are promising, the experiments are performed on CodeT5, which, while capable, isn't a current generation LLM. Further work must scale the approach to such LLMs
    *   **Implicit Assumption of Static Code:** The homoglyph substitution approach is inherently reliant on the assumption that the code is stored in a relatively unaltered format. Compilation or further transformations could remove the character level embedding. The strategy might not generalize directly to obfuscated code.
    *   **Limited to code:** It is not clear if the use of the attention mechanism and homoglyphs can be applied to watermarking in non-code domains.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and stealthy watermarking techniques for generative code models. The attention mechanism-based embedding position selection and homoglyph substitution combined with distributed triggers could become standard techniques in this area. However, the broader impact will depend on how well the approach scales to larger models and handles more sophisticated adversarial attacks.

**Justification for Score:**

I am giving this paper a score of 7. The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing the limitations of existing watermarking methods for code models. The proposed method achieves high verification rates, minimal impact on task performance, and superior stealth. The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, and the paper clearly articulates its contributions. However, the paper's novelty is incremental, building upon existing techniques. The experiments should include more aggressive adversarial techniques to validate stealthiness, and there are questions about its scalability to larger models and to more complicated code formats. The influence is currently limited by these points.
